        Known accepted debt grouped by subsystem:
        {
  "are-the-proposed-changes-technically-correct": [
    {
      "id": "DEBT-010",
      "concern": "are the proposed changes technically correct?: psycopg3 cursor.execute(sql_text) does support multi-statement strings when no parameters are passed, but the plan should test against a `do $$ ... end $$;` block to confirm psycopg3's multi-statement handling preserves pl/pgsql block boundaries. test m's coverage (step 15) should include a fixture file with a do block to verify, not just create table / create function.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [
        "capacity-reconciler-20260513"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "DEBT-011",
      "concern": "are the proposed changes technically correct?: backoff reset condition: invariant 5 says 'successful spawn for that (pool, route_key) resets the counter to 0.' plan step 5.1 defines consecutive_spawn_failures as 'count of consecutive failures since the most recent successful promotion-to-active for (pool, route_key).' that's a different signal \u2014 a worker's spawn-attempt succeeds when the pod is created (current spawn_failed surface), but 'promotion to active' is the lifecycle completion. these are minutes apart. the brief's reset condition wants reset-on-successful-pod-creation; the plan's wants reset-on-promotion-to-active, which delays reset and overcounts failures during the initialization window. probably the planner's choice is better (more conservative), but the deviation from the brief should be acknowledged.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [
        "capacity-reconciler-20260513"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "DEBT-012",
      "concern": "are the proposed changes technically correct?: authoritative-mode active-scale-down still consults the legacy desired formula via _execute_scaling.max_by_capacity = decision.workers_to_terminate, derived from worker_state.py:616-627's legacy desired = max(min_based, task_based, buffer_based). this is one of the two conflicting formulas the brief identifies as root cause. plan should justify or replace the input.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [
        "capacity-reconciler-20260513"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "DEBT-013",
      "concern": "are the proposed changes technically correct?: shadow admit exercises stale backoff state. step 5 says shadow act() must not update backoff state, but admit still consults next_spawn_allowed_at. with no shadow-side backoff state, suppression decisions never appear pre-cutover; brief's '>=50 meaningful capacity events including backoff suppressions' becomes structurally unobservable.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [
        "capacity-reconciler-20260513"
      ]
    }
  ],

    {
      "id": "DEBT-007",
      "concern": "did the work fully address the issue hints, user notes, and approved plan requirements?: plan structure warnings from validator say 'each step section should include at least one numbered substep' and 'each step section should reference at least one file in backticks'. several v4 step sections (step 6, step 7, step 9, step 10, step 11, step 12, step 13, step 14, step 16, step 17) say only 'identical to v3 step n' without re-listing substeps or files, which is what triggered the validator warnings. the plan is harder to execute standalone if the executor doesn't have v3 in front of them. process-level concern; doesn't change correctness.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,

      "concern": "did the work fully address the issue hints, user notes, and approved plan requirements?: brief phase b item 2 makes the captured-incident replay (test f) the non-negotiable proof of correctness. plan step 10 explicitly synthesizes the fixture from the postmortem shape unless a real snapshot is provided, weakening the gate signal at cutover.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,

      ]
    },

      "id": "DEBT-009",
      "concern": "did the work fully address the issue hints, user notes, and approved plan requirements?: invariant 7 (record() before any spawn side effect) combined with the plan's unique (pool, cycle_id) constraint and step 6's append-only insert-based backoff updates contradict each other: a second insert for the same (pool, cycle_id) after a runpod failure violates the unique constraint, so the backoff-state update path cannot land in production as written. constraint must be relaxed or backoff state must live in a separate table.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [

      ]
    }

      "occurrence_count": 1,

  "find-the-callers-of-the-changed-function-what-arguments-do-they-actually-pass-does-the-fix-handle-all-of-them": [
    {
      "id": "DEBT-016",
      "concern": "find the callers of the changed function. what arguments do they actually pass? does the fix handle all of them?: step 9 says spawn_one_worker takes an optional intent_row_id and writes it under workers.metadata. the existing _spawn_worker builds a metadata dict at control_loop.py:1392-1404 with fields including runpod_id, termination_reason on failure, ssh_details, etc. the plan should call out that the new field is additive and doesn't collide with these keys, and that the worker creation path (db.create_worker_record at control_loop.py:1376) accepts the metadata at the right point.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [
        "capacity-reconciler-20260513"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "DEBT-017",
      "concern": "find the callers of the changed function. what arguments do they actually pass? does the fix handle all of them?: reconciler's act() in authoritative mode calls runpod.terminate_worker(runpod_id) for cancelpendingspawn. existing caller in control_loop.py:410 wraps this with metadata update + summary.workers_terminated += 1. the plan describes equivalent metadata update in step 5.4 but doesn't address the summary/stats integration \u2014 cyclesummary fields like workers_terminated, workers_spawned are read by health_monitor and structured logs. if the reconciler bypasses the summary, those counters under-report.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [
        "capacity-reconciler-20260513"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "DEBT-018",
      "concern": "find the callers of the changed function. what arguments do they actually pass? does the fix handle all of them?: reconciler's cancelpendingspawn db update uses `db.update_worker_status(worker_id, 'terminated', {'termination_reason': 'reconciler_cancel_pending_spawn', 'terminated_at': ..., 'intent_row_id': ...})`. existing equivalent in control_loop.py:416-420 also calls `await self.runpod.terminate_worker(ws.runpod_id)` before `db.update_worker_status`. v4 step 8.5 mirrors this ordering. the plan doesn't address what happens if runpod.terminate_worker fails \u2014 the existing code at control_loop.py:413-414 logs a warning and proceeds with the db update anyway. the reconciler should match that resilience pattern; plan should make this explicit so the executor doesn't accidentally abort the cycle on runpod terminate failure.",
      "occurrence_count": 1,
      "plan_ids": [
        "capacity-reconciler-20260513"
      ]
    }
  ],

    {

      "concern": "search for related code that handles the same concept. is the reported issue a symptom of something broader?: the new auto-discovery migration runner now spans the entire repo's sql/ directory, not just the four files in this pr. this widens the implicit contract: any future sql file dropped into sql/ will be auto-applied at next deploy, including files added by other prs or merge conflicts that an operator might miss. existing manually-driven workflow (supabase sql editor copy-paste) had a human gatekeeper. the plan does not propose a review checklist or pr-template gate around new sql/ files. minor process risk worth surfacing.",

        Robustness level:
        standard

        Requirements:
        - Decide exactly one of: PROCEED, ITERATE, ESCALATE, TIEBREAKER.
        - Use the weighted score, flag details (including `evidence`), plan delta, recurring critiques, and preflight results as judgment context.
        - PROCEED when execution should move forward now.
        - ITERATE when revising the plan is the best next move.
        - ESCALATE when the loop is stuck, churn is recurring, or user intervention is needed.
        - TIEBREAKER when a flag group reflects an *unresolvable constraint tension* (architectural or philosophical — requires a human call) rather than a plan-quality issue. Use TIEBREAKER only when the Iteration Pressure Analysis shows `addressed_then_reopened_count >= 2` for a fuzzy group OR the group has >=2 member flags across >=2 iterations. If the concern is simply that the plan writer hasn't tried hard enough, use ITERATE instead. When recommending TIEBREAKER you MUST provide `tiebreaker_question` (the decision question for human resolution), `tiebreaker_flag_ids` (which flags this resolves), and `tiebreaker_fuzzy_group_id` (which group this resolves). Cite specific flag IDs and iterations in your rationale.
        - `signals_assessment`: one paragraph summarizing score trajectory, flag status, and preflight posture.

        Flags come in two tiers:
        - **Blocking** (severity = significant/likely-significant): These are serious concerns. If you recommend PROCEED, you MUST provide a `flag_resolutions` entry for every blocking flag. There is no implicit acceptance.
        - **Noted** (everything else): Acknowledge in your rationale but they don't block PROCEED.

        If there are blocking flags and you want to PROCEED, provide `flag_resolutions` with one entry per blocking flag. If you cannot resolve every blocking flag, choose ITERATE (send back for revision) or ESCALATE (human intervention needed).
        Structurally unresolvable flags (for example, infrastructure outside the repo or product decisions that require a human) are ESCALATE, not PROCEED with a non-answer.

        For each blocking flag:
        - **dispute**: The critique is factually wrong. Evidence must cite something specific (file path, line, API doc, etc.). Generic statements like "handled correctly" are invalid.
        - **accept_tradeoff**: The concern is real but intentionally accepted as a known limitation. Rationale must be specific to this flag. Boilerplate like "acceptable within scope" is invalid.
        - Schema requirement: every `flag_resolutions` entry must include both `evidence` and `rationale`. Use `""` for the field that does not apply to that action.

        If there are no blocking flags, return `flag_resolutions: []`.
        Always return `accepted_tradeoffs`; use `[]` when none apply.

        Populate `settled_decisions` with design choices that should carry into review without re-litigation. Return `[]` when there are none.

        Example:
        ```json
        {
          "recommendation": "PROCEED",
          "rationale": "Core fix is correct. Convention concern accepted.",
          "signals_assessment": "Score stable at 2.5, preflight passed, no recurring critiques.",
          "warnings": ["Verify edge case with composite moduli during execution."],
          "flag_resolutions": [
            {"flag_id": "correctness-1", "action": "dispute", "evidence": "allow_migrate and allow_migrate_model produce identical behavior for this use case (verified at django/db/utils.py:286).", "rationale": ""},
            {"flag_id": "performance-1", "action": "accept_tradeoff", "evidence": "", "rationale": "Cold-start latency remains 40ms above target because the cache warmup job is owned by platform and outside this repo; rollout is still approved for the limited internal beta."},
            {"flag_id": "conventions-1", "action": "accept_tradeoff", "evidence": "", "rationale": "Minor naming inconsistency is confined to this helper and would create churn across generated fixtures; track it as follow-up cleanup instead of blocking this fix."}
          ],
          "accepted_tradeoffs": [],
          "settled_decisions": []
        }
        ```

This is operationally safer for a production controller, though it leaves deployment discipline outside app startup."}],"settled_decisions":[{"id":"pool_scoped_capacity","decision":"Capacity planning, min/max bounds, idle buffer, and authoritative locking are pool-scoped.","rationale":"The repository config exposes pool/orchestrator-level capacity limits, so route keys must not create independent capacity budgets."},{"id":"route_scoped_backoff","decision":"Route keys are used for demand attribution, audit, and `(pool, route_key)` spawn backoff inside the pool budget.","rationale":"This preserves per-route RunPod failure isolation without overprovisioning the pool."},{"id":"non_null_pool_floor_route_key","decision":"Use `__pool_floor__` as the non-null sentinel for pool-floor/buffer spawn backoff rows.","rationale":"PostgreSQL can then enforce uniqueness with `PRIMARY KEY (pool, route_key)` without nullable-key ambiguity."},{"id":"three_slice_rollout","decision":"Ship as shadow, authoritative cutover, then final cleanup slices with separate gates.","rationale":"This keeps production safety and rollback while still satisfying final deletion criteria after the observation window."}],"tiebreaker_question":"","tiebreaker_flag_ids":[],"tiebreaker_fuzzy_group_id":""}
